**TIME:** 11:02 AM PST  
**DATE:** Tuesday, December 9th, 2024  
**LOCATION:** Cold Steel Manufacturing Floor, Ventura, CA

---

You smile and follow Lynn through the double doors.

---

## **THE MANUFACTURING FLOOR**

The sound hits you first.

---

*Grinding. Hammering. The hiss of hydraulics. The roar of industrial fans.*

---

The space is massive.

High ceilings. Concrete floors. Industrial lighting casting everything in harsh white.

Rows of workstations. Conveyor systems. Quality control stations.

And *heat*.

You can feel it from the ovens even from here.

---

**LYNN:**

He has to raise his voice over the machinery.

"This is where it all happens. Steel comes in as raw blanks from our suppliers. Leaves as finished blades."

---

He leads you down the main aisle.

---

### **STATION 1: BLANK CUTTING**

Large hydraulic presses.

Workers feeding sheet steel into dies.

---

**CLANG. CLANG. CLANG.**

Each press stamps out a blade blank.

Rough shape. No edge. No finish.

---

**LYNN:**

"We stamp most of our production blades. Faster than forging for volume work. The steel is already good quality—1055, 1060, 1095 depending on the product. We're not trying to make Damascus or anything fancy. Just functional steel."

---

He picks up a fresh blank from a bin.

Hands it to you.

"Feel that. Still has the burrs from the stamp. Rough. Ugly."

---

You run your thumb along the edge.

Not sharp. Just a pressed profile.

---

**LYNN:**

"By the time it leaves here, that'll be a Gladius. Or a kukri. Or a machete. Depends on the order queue."

---

### **STATION 2: GRINDING & PROFILING**

Belt grinders.

Workers in safety glasses and hearing protection.

Sparks flying as rough blanks get their bevels ground.

---

**LYNN:**

"This is where we establish the edge geometry. Convex grinds, flat grinds, hollow grinds—depends on the blade. Most of our machetes get convex because it's stronger and easier to maintain in the field."

---

He points to a worker carefully guiding a kukri blank along a large belt grinder.

"See how he's working the curve? That's not automated. That's *skill*. You can't just feed a kukri through a machine and expect it to come out right."

---

### **STATION 3: HEAT TREATMENT**

Massive industrial ovens.

Temperature gauges reading 1,500°F+.

---

**LYNN:**

"This is where the magic happens. Metallurgical magic, anyway."

He gestures to the ovens.

"We heat the blades to critical temperature—around 1,475°F for 1060 carbon steel. Hold it there until the steel's crystal structure changes. Then we quench it in oil."

---

He points to large quench tanks.

"Fast cooling locks in the hardness. Makes the steel tough. But also brittle."

---

"So then we temper it—reheat it to a lower temp, around 400-450°F. That relieves internal stress, adds flexibility. You want a blade that's hard enough to hold an edge but flexible enough not to shatter."

---

A pause.

---

"Get the heat treatment wrong, and it doesn't matter how good your grind is. The blade will fail."

---

### **STATION 4: HANDLE ASSEMBLY**

Rows of finished blades.

Workers attaching handles—wood, micarta, Kraton rubber, G10.

Tang pins being hammered. Pommels threaded. Guards fitted.

---

**LYNN:**

"This is where personality comes in. A blade without a handle is just a sharp piece of metal. The handle is what makes it a *tool*."

---

He picks up a finished Trail Hawk from a rack.

"Feel this."

---

You take it.

The hickory is smooth but not slippery. The balance is perfect.

---

**LYNN:**

"That handle was shaped by hand. Sanded. Sealed. Fitted to the head with a steel wedge. It'll outlast most people."

---

### **STATION 5: SHARPENING & FINAL QC**

Workers at whetstones and mechanical sharpeners.

Each blade tested.

Paper cuts. Rope cuts. Visual inspection for defects.

---

**LYNN:**

"Every blade that leaves here gets sharpened to our standard. Not razor-sharp—that's too fragile for hard use. But sharp enough to cut paper cleanly and hold an edge through abuse."

---

He picks up a finished machete.

Draws it across a piece of printer paper.

Clean slice.

---

"That's the Cold Steel standard."

---

---

## **11:18 AM PST**

Sarah appears at the edge of the manufacturing floor.

She's holding something.

---

**SARAH:**

"Lynn! Found it!"

---

She walks over.

In her hands: a black cordura sheath.

And inside—

---

**THE CORSICAN**

7-inch blade.

Double-edged.

Needle point.

The steel has a slight patina—not rust, just age.

The handle is black micarta. Simple. Functional.

---

**LYNN:**

He takes it from Sarah.

Draws the blade.

Inspects it.

---

"1997 production. One of the last runs before we discontinued it."

He tests the edge with his thumb.

"Needs sharpening. But the steel is sound."

---

He hands it to you.

---

"Let's get this on the sharpening line. Five minutes. Then it's yours."

---

The Corsican is in your hand.

Cold. Balanced. *Lethal*.

**TOMMY** (late 40s, safety glasses, leather apron):

---

**LYNN:**

"Ryan here wants to sharpen his Corsican himself. Can you walk him through it?"

---

Tommy wipes his hands on his apron.

---

He gestures to an empty station.

Bottles of honing compound.

**TOMMY:**

"Alright, Mr. Lindale. First rule: *respect the wheel*. It's spinning at 1,200 RPM. It'll take off more metal than you think, faster than you think."

---

He takes the Corsican from you.

Holds it up to the light.

---

"Good blade. 1095 carbon steel, if I remember right. Takes an edge like a dream, but it's also unforgiving. You go too aggressive, you'll ruin the geometry."

---

He hands it back.

---

"Here's what we're going to do. Double-edged blade, so you've got two edges to worry about. We're going for a *convex* edge—slight curve to the bevel. Stronger than a V-edge, easier to maintain."

---

He demonstrates with his hands.

---

"You're going to hold the blade at about a 20-degree angle to the wheel. Light pressure. Let the wheel do the work. Move the blade in smooth passes from ricasso to tip. Don't dwell in one spot or you'll create a flat spot."

---

He points to the wheel.

---

"And most importantly—keep it cool. If the steel gets too hot, you'll draw the temper out and ruin the hardness. If you see sparks turning blue or the metal changing color, dip it in water immediately."

---

He steps back.

---

"You've got maybe eight minutes before your ride shows up. Let's see what you can do."

---

**LYNN:**

He's watching.

Arms crossed.

Smiling.

"This is going to be good."

---
